QUESTIONS PRESENTED 


This case presents the question of when the six-year 
statute of limitations set forth in the Tucker Act, 28 U.S.C. 
§ 2501, begins to run where the federal government, by 
statute, attempts to modify its contracts as it did in the 
Winstar and Mobil cases. The specific issues presented are: 


1. Whether the government can eliminate contract 
rights ipse dixit through the enactment of legislation 
aimed at relieving the government of its contractual 
obligations, so that an aggrieved party’s contract claim 
accrues immediately when a statute is passed 
announcing that the government might not perform its 
future contract obligations, rather than at the later date 
when the government actually fails to perform? 


2. Whether the “as-applied” taking claims of 
property owners participating in a government housing 
program accrued immediately upon the enactment of 
legislation that expressly left open the possibility that the 
owners still would be permitted to leave the program 
and use their properties as they desired, rather than at 
the later date when the government applied the 
legislation to render a decision on the permitted use of 


the properties?

TEXT OF STATUTES AND REGULATIONS 


The pertinent text of the statutes and regulations at issue 
in this matter is reproduced in the Appendix (“App.”) 
attached hereto. See App. A71-105. The provisions most 
significant to the disposition of this petition are as follows: 


28 U.S.C. § 2501 Time for filing suit 


Every claim of which the United States Court of Federal 
claims has jurisdiction shall be barred unless the petition 
thereon is filed within six years after such claim first accrues. 


42 U.S.C. § 1472(c) Prepayment and refinancing provisions 


” * 


(4)(A) Agreement by borrower to extend low income use. 


Before accepting any offer to prepay, or requesting 
refinancing in accordance with subsection (b)(3) of this 
section of, any loan made or insured under section 1484 or 
1485 of this title pursuant to a contract entered into prior to 
December 15, 1989, the Secretary shall make reasonable 
efforts to enter into an agreement with the borrower under 
which the borrower will make a binding commitment to 
extend the low income use of the assisted housing and 
related facilities involved for not less than the 20-year period 
beginning on the date on which the agreement is executed. 


* * * 


(5)(A) Offer to sell to nonprofit organizations and public 
agencies 
(i) In general 
If the Secretary determines after a reasonable period that 
an agreement will not be entered into with a borrower under 


paragraph (4), the Secretary shall require the borrower 
(except as provided in subparagraph (G)) to offer to sell the 
assisted housing and related facilities . . . 


xill 


(G) Exception 


This paragraph shall not apply to any offer to prepay, or 
any request to refinance in accordance with subsection (b)(3) 
of this section, any loan made or insured under section 1484 
or 1485 of this title pursuant to a contract entered into prior 
to December 15, 1989, if - 


* * * 


(ii) the Secretary determines that housing 
opportunities of minorities will not be materially affected as 
a result of the prepayment or refinancing, and that - 


(I) the borrower (and any successor in interest 
thereof) are obligated to ensure that tenants of the housing 
and related facilities financed with the loan will not be 
displaced due to a change in the use of the housing, or to an 
increase in rental or other charges, as a result of the 
prepayment or refinancing; or 


(II) there is an adequate supply of safe, decent, 
and affordable rental housing within the market area of the 
housing and related facilities and sufficient actions have 
been taken to ensure that the rental housing will be made 
available to each tenant upon displacement.

XV 


CONSOLIDATED PETITION FOR CERTIORARI 


Petitioners Franconia Associates, et al., and Grass Valley 
Terrace, et al. (“Petitioners”), respectfully petition this Court 
for a writ of certiorari to review two decisions of the United 
States Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit. 


OPINIONS BELOW 


Franconia Associates v. United States, No. 97-381C, 43 Fed. 
Cl. 702 (May 9, 1999), recons. denied, 44 Fed. Cl. 315 (July 21, 
1999), affd, No. 99-5123, 240 F.3d 1358 (Fed. Cir. Feb. 15, 
2001), reh. denied (Fed. Cir. June 12, 2001). 


Grass Valley Terrace v. United States, No. 98-726C, 46 Fed. 
Cl. 629 (Apr. 12, 2000), aff'd, No. 00-5094 (Fed. Cir. May 17, 
2001) (per curium). 


JURISDICTION 


This petition seeks review of two closely-related 
decisions of the Federal Circuit. The Federal Circuit issued 
an opinion in Franconia Associates v. United States 
(“Franconia”) on February 15, 2001. Petitioners then timely 
filed a Petition for Rehearing En Banc, which the Federal 
Circuit denied on June 12, 2001. In the interim, the Federal 
Circuit issued a per curium decision in Grass Valley Terrace v. 
United States (“Grass Valley”) on May 17, 2001. 


This Court has jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. § 1254(1). On 
July 27, 2001, Petitioners timely filed an application for an 
extension of time, requesting permission to file a 
Consolidated Petition for Writ of Certiorari addressing both 
cases on September 10, 2001. The Court granted Petitioners’ 
application by Order dated July 31, 2001, extending the filing 
deadline in Grass Valley until September 14, 2001. 


STATUTES AND REGULATIONS INVOLVED 


Tucker Act, 28 U.S.C. § 2501 (1994) (App. 82); 42 US.C. 
§ 1472(c) (1994) (App. 83-90); 7 C.F.R. § 1965.215 (2000) (App. 


l 


91-105); Emergency Low Income Housing Preservation Act, 
Pub. L. No. 100-242, tit. II, 101 Stat. 1877 (1988) (App. 71-81). 


STATEMENT OF THE CASE 
Il. Overview 


Petitioners are owners of properties used as affordable 
housing in rural areas of the United States. The housing was 
financed Sy mortgages issued by the Farmer's Home 
Administration (“FmHA”), ! an agency of the Department of 
Agriculture, pursuant to Section 515 of the Housing Act of 
1949 (the “Section 515 program”). According to contracts 
executed by each owner upon entering into the Section 515 
program and regulations then in effect, the owners hau the 
option to prepay their mortgages at any time during their 
fifty-year mortgage terms. Upon prepayment, the owners 
could terminate their participation in the program and use 
their buildings as market-rent properties. 


So long as the properties remained in the Section 515 
program, the owners could earn only a marginal annual 
return representing a small percentage of their initial down 
payment. As a result, Petitioners intended from the very 
beginning to prepay their mortgages as soon as 
circumstances would allow in order to realize the full 
economic potential of their properties. Without the ability to 
terminate their participation in the program at some time 
before each of their full mortgage terms expired, none of the 
Petitioners ever would have entered into the program. 


In 1988, however, Congress passed a law setting up a 
new scheme for determining whether FmHA would have to 
approve requests by owners to prepay. The new regime 
only allows an owner to prepay without any restrictions if 
the agency determines in its discretion that, among other 


1 Although the agency formerly known as the FmHA is now known as 
to the agency by its original name. 


things, there is no longer a need for affordable housing in 
the area where the property is located. If the agency cannot 
make this determination, an owner desiring to leave the 
program must either agree to remain in the program for an 
extended period of time or risk losing the property through 
a forced sale to a public or non-profit entity. 

Each of the owners in this action decided at some point 
following the passage of this legislation to exercise its 
prepayment option and leave the Section 515 program. 
However, none of them could, upon their intended 
prepayment dates, meet the statutory exception allowing the 
agency to accept requests to prepay without any restrictions. 
As a result, Petitioners remain trapped in the program and 
subject to its severe income restrictions with no reasonable 
means of escaping before their full fifty-year mortgage terms 
expire. Plaintiffs therefore brought suit, alleging counts for 
breach of contract and a taking of their property. 


The Federal Circuit held in Franconia? that Petitioners’ 
claims were barred by the six-year statute of limitations set 
forth in the Tucker Act, finding that both the contract claims 
and the taking claims accrued automatically on the date that 
the legislation was enacted. The decision was premised on a 
number of fundamental errors of law, including: 


* The court exempted the government from ordinary 
contract law principles by holding that (1) the legislation, 
which was specifically targeted at relieving the government 
from its obligations under the contracts, immediately 
“eliminated” Petitioners’ contract rights, (2) the legislation 
constituted an immediate breach of Petitioners’ prepayment 
option, even though the contracts entitled owners to exercise 


2 ~The other decision at issue, Grass Valley, was a per curiam decision 
issued by the Federal Circuit following Franconia. The Grass Valley 
decision affirmed the dismissal, on limitations grounds, of contract claims 
that are materially identical to the contract claims in Francoma. Because 
the Federal Circuit did not issue a written opinion in Grass Valley, the 
discussion herein is focused on the court's analysis in Franconia. 


3 


this option at any time during their full mortgage terms, and 
(3) Petitioners’ prepayment option imposed no reciprocal 
obligation on the government to accept prepayment requests 
made by owners, thus rendering the contracts illusory; and 

* The court failed to recognize that, because the 
legislation expressly left open the possibility that owners 
would be permitted by the agency to prepay and use their 
properties as they desired, each owner's taking claim did not 
accrue until the FmHA applied the legislation by rendering a 
decision on the owner's prepayment request. 

The Federal Circuit in effect held that every Section 515 
owner in the country who might desire to prepay at some 
point during their fifty-year mortgage terms was required to 
bring suit within six years after the enactment of the statute 
in February 1988 on pain of having their claims time-barred, 
regardless of whether they had any intention of prepaying 
during that window of time. The decision thus contravenes 
the established precedent of this Court and wreaks havoc on 
settled principles of law. The decision also disposes of the 
claims of hundreds of housing owners who invested in the 
program based on express government commitments and 
who are now stranded in the program as a result of the 
government's failure to live up to its obligations. The 
decision extinguishes these owners’ claims only because 
they failed to file suit prematurely. 


Il. Statement of Facts 
A. The Section 515 Program 


Beginning in the 1960s, the federal government 
established several programs encouraging private citizens to 
construct, own, and manage federally-assisted affordable 
housing units. See Cohen & Mattis, Prepayment Rights: 
Abrogation by the Low-Income Housing Preservation and 
Resident Homeownership Act of 1990, 28 REAL. PROP. PROB. & 
TR. J. 1, 2-4 (1993). The so-called “Section 515” program at 


issue in this case was one such program. Section 515 of the 
Housing Act of 1949 (codified at 42 U.S.C. § 1485), added to 
the Act in 1962, authorized the FmHA to enter into contracts 
with private owners to make loans for the purpose of 
developing housing for low- and moderate-income and 
elderly tenants in rural areas. Owners retained titled to their 
properties while in the program, but were permitted to 
collect only a minimal annual return on their initial 
investment.? App. A170. The owners also were required to 
accept only eligible persons as tenants, to charge no higher 
rents than permitted by FmHA, to maintain cash reserves, 
and to comply with certain regulations not inconsistent with 
the terms of their contracts. App. A169-72, A177, A181. 
Mortgage loans under the program had terms of up to 
fifty years. App. A165, A176. Obviously, few owners would 
be willing or able to accept the income restrictions required 
by the program for such a long period of time, which likely 
would not even expire within their own lifetimes. Although 
certain tax benefits initially were available under the 
program, those benefits ran out as each project's 
depreciation schedule expired. See 125 Cong. Rec. 18204 
(1979) (“The tax advantage in this type of project looks 
toward a maximum term of about 10 years.”). Thus, unless 
owners could capitalize on their long-term investment in the 
housing units at some point during the normal useful life of 
those units, the strict limitations on the properties’ income 
potential would have made the projects uneconomical and 


3 The return permitted under the program was 8% of the owners’ 
“initial investment” ir: the property, 1.c., its down payment, rather than 8% 
of its equity in the property or its market value. App. A170. For example, 
an owner that made a $91,237 down payment on a project with a 
$1,238,000 mortgage would earn only a maximum of $7,299 per year so 
long as it remained in the program. Id. Assuming that after twenty years 
the owner built up $1,000,000 of equity in the property and that the 
market value of the property as a commercial apartment complex had 
risen to $2,000,000, the owner would still receive at most only $7,299 per 
year, which would amount to only 0.73% of its equity and only 0.36% of 
the property's market value. 


no reasonable investor would have participated in the 
program. Something more was required to induce owners 
to enter into the program. 


That “something more” was the termination option - the 
government's assurance that an owner could escape these 
income restrictions and convert the property to conventional 
market-rate housing by paying off the loan before the end of 
its term. Pursuant to the discretion conferred on it by 
Congress, the FmHA included the following provision in the 
promissory note executed by each owner: 


Prepayments of scheduled installments, or any 
portion thereof, may be made at any time at the 
option of Borrower. 


App. A176. This provision reflected contemporaneous 
FmHA regulations. See 7 C.F.R. § 1822.95(c) (1978). 


The termination option was a critical component of the 
Section 515 program from the perspective of both the 
owners and the government. None of the Petitioners would 
have entered into the program or signed its contract absent 
the right to prepay and leave the program at some time 
before the expiration of its mortgage term, at which point 
each owner could convert to market rents or sell the 
property based on its commercial market value. Petitioners 
thus entered into the program with specific long-term 
investment goals that could not be achieved absent the right 
to prepay and terminate their participation in the program. 
See Cienega Gardens v. United States, 38 Fed. Cl. 64, 75 (1997) 
(finding that under a similar housing program, prepayment 
served as one of the “primary incentives,” an “essential 
aspect” of the program, and an “enticement” without which 
the owners would not have entered into the program), rev'd 
on other grounds, 162 F.3d 1123 (Fed. Cir. 1998). 4 


4 Petitioners did not, as the courts below assumed, invest in the Section 
515 program to obtain a low-interest rate loan. See App. A3, Al7, A46. 


6 


From the government's perspective, the ability of owners 
to prepay and ultimately use their properties as market-rate 
investments served as a chief inducement to recruit private 
parties to enter into the program. Id; Belden & Wiener, 
HOUSING IN RURAL AMERICA 122 (noting that the Section 515 
program was “predicated on the assumption that private, 
profit-motivated owners could be enticed to build, own and 
operate low-income rental housing” and that “prepayment 
is wholly consistent” with this strategy). Further, the money 
received through prepayment could be used to finance 
additional rural housing under the program. See 42 Op. 
Att’y Gen. 417, 419 (Dec. 29, 1969). { 


B. Subsequent Legislation 


Not surprisingly, as time passed, many owners began to 
exercise their termination option, prepaying their loans to 
realize the investment returns to which their contracts 
entitled them. See H.R. Rep. No. 96-154, 96th Cong., 1st Sess. 
at 43 (1979), reprinted in 1979 U.S.C.C.A.N. at 2359. The 
FmHA maintained that it was contractually required to 
accept such prepayments despite concern that doing so 
would significantly reduce the supply of affordable housing. 
Id. (noting that FmHA “believes it must honor requests to 
prepay or refinance these loans”).5 To curb this potential 


The effective interest rates on the loans (which were not in fact low, see, 
e.g., App. A176) provided the owners no benefit whatsoever, because the 
only profit owners could receive while in the program was a minimal 
annual return on their initial investment. The true beneficiaries of the 


government subsidies that produced an effectively low interest rate are 
the tenants, in that the government subsidies permit rents to remain low. 


5 Congress initially responded by passing a statute in 1979 that 
restricted the FmHA’s authority to accept prepayments of Section 515 


loans. Pub. L. No. 96-153, § 503, 93 Stat. 1134 (1979). In response to the 
political uproar caused by this repudiation of the owners’ rights, however, 
Congress repealed the statute in 1980 and restored the termination option 
for loans issued prior to 1979. The 1980 legislation also required new 
owners to agree to operate their properties as low-income housing for a 
minimum of 15 or 20 years. Pub. L. No. 96-399, § 514, 94 Stat. 1671 (1980). 


7 


loss of housing, Congress imposed a series of moratoriums 
from 1986 through 1988 greatly restricting the ability of 
Section 515 owners to prepay their mortgages. Before the 
last moratorium expired, on February 5, 1988 Congress 
enacted the Emergency Low Income Housing Preservation 
Act, Pub. L. No. 100-242, tit. II, 101 Stat. 1877 (1988), codified 
as amended at 42 U.S.C. § 1472(c) (“ELIHPA”) (App. A71-81). 


Whereas the relevant contracts and regulations obligated 
the FmHA to accept prepayment requests made by owners 
“at any time at [their] option,” ELIHPA drastically limited 
the circumstances under which the agency could accept such 
requests. First, ELIHPA prohibits the FmHA from accepting 
any prepayment until it has attempted to secure the owner's 
agreement to give up the termination option and extend the 
low-income use of the property for at least an additional 
twenty years. 42 U.S.C. § 1472(c)(4)(A) (App. A83). 


Second, if an owner will not surrender its termination 
option and agree to extend the low-income use of the 
property, ELIHPA compels the owner, before exercising that 
option, to offer the property for sale to any qualified 
nonprofit organization or public agency for a six month 
period. Id. § 1472(c)(5). A buyer can be “qualified” by the 
FmHA only if it agrees to maintain the low-income use of 
the property, and no sale can be completed unless the 
agency approves the purchase price. Id. Although a hapless 
owner theoretically could prepay at the end of this “forced- 
sale” period if no “qualified” buyer emerges, the owner first 
has to gamble that its property will not be snatched away at 
a price not determined on the open market. 


This change in the governing law is the basis for the two classes of 
plaintiffs in the original actions below - owners with claims based on pre- 
1979 contracts, whose original termination rights were restored by the 
1980 legislation, and owners with claims based on post-1979 contracts, 
whose termination rights have always been exercisable only after a 
minimum of 15 or 20 years. Petitioners are all “pre-1979” owners, due to 
the fact that ELIHPA addressed only pre-1979 contracts and, as a result, 
only the pre-1979 claims were dismissed by the lower courts as untimely. 


Third, if an owner is unwilling either to extend its 
participation in the program or subject itself to the six- 
month forced-sale process, ELIHPA permits an owner to 
prepay only if he or she agrees to maintain the low-income 
rental rates until the current tenants choose to move out. Id. 
§ 1472(c)(5)(G)(ii)(I) (App. A89). The relevant regulations 
make clear that by agreeing to this restriction, an owner 
cannot require a tenant to move out, other than for cause, 
until “the end of the remaining useful life of the project.” 
7 C.F.R. Pt. 1965, Subpart E (1997), Ex. A-4. 


ELIHPA also provides one exception that, while narrow, 
is key to the outcome of this matter. If the FmHA makes a 
discretionary decision that there is no longer a need for the 
housing in the area where it is located, and that housing 
opportunities for minorities will not be adversely affected, 
the owner can prepay and leave the program with no strings 
attached, consistent with the terms of its contract and the 
contemporaneous regulations. 42 U.S.C. § 1472(c)(5)(G)(ii) 
(App. A89). Thus, the statute does not in all cases prohibit 
withdrawal from the program. Instead, the agency must 
accept an owner's request to prepay without restriction if the 
statutory conditions are satisfied when the request is made. ¢ 


C. Application of the Legislation 


Following passage of ELIHPA, the FmHA denied 
applications submitted by various Petitioners requesting to 
prepay their mortgages without any restrictions. App. A129. 
Other Petitioners who decided to prepay were informed by 
the agency and others that, based on then-existing market 
conditions in the area of their properties, any prepayment 
request would be denied under the requirements of the new 
statute, and that submitting a request therefore would be 


* The prepayment restrictions imposed by ELIHPA on pre-1979 
contracts, which were only “interim” in nature (see ELIHPA § 
202(a)(9),(10)), were made permanent - and extended to post-1979 
contracts - by another statute which was passed on October 28, 1992. Pub. 
L. No. 102-550, §§ 2, 712, 106 Stat. 3681, 3841 (1992). 


9 


futile. Jd. Thus, none of the Petitioners was able, at the time 
it desired to exercise its termination option, to satisfy the 
statutory conditions for prepaying without restrictions. 
Consequently, Petitioners remain stranded in the Section 515 
program and subject to its severe income restrictions to this 
day, and likely will remain stranded until their fifty-year 
mortgage terms expire, despite their express desire to 
prepay and convert to market rents as soon as their personal 
circumstances and market conditions would allow. 


Petitioners filed suit in the Court of Federal Claims in 
1997 (Franconia) and 1998 (Grass Valiey) asserting two counts: 
(1) a contract claim alleging that ELIHPA constituted an 
anticipatory repudiation of their option to prepay their 
mortgages at any time, and (2) a taking claim alleging that 
the legislation, as applied to their properties, effected 
takings of their real property interests. App. A132. To date, 
no discovery has taken place on any of Petitioners’ claims. 


III. Proceedings Below 
A. Proceedings Before the Court of Federal Claims 


The Court of Federal Claims issued its decision in 
Franconia Associates v. United States on May 6, 1999, in which 
it granted the government's motion to dismiss Petitioners’ 
contract claims on limitations grounds. The court held that 
ELIHPA constituted an immediate breach of Petitioners’ 
contracts, rather than an anticipatory repudiation, and that 
their contract claims thus accrued on the date that the 
agency issued regulations implementing the statute. App. 
A28-29. To reach this conclusion, the court found that the 
prepayment clause of Petitioners’ contracts imposed no duty 
of performance on the government whatsoever: 


A careful reading of this clause reveals that no 
performance by the government is required - it is 
merely a promise to allow the borrower to prepay at 
any time during the life of the loan (emph. added). 


10 


App. A31; see also A32 (“[T]here was no performance due by 
the government under the contract.”). The court concluded 
that the contracts imposed on the government nothing more 
than a continuing obligation to “keep its promise,” and thus 
the contracts were breached when the government ceased 
keeping its promise by changing the law. Id. 


Although the government did not move to dismiss 
Petitioners’ taking claims, and although there had been no 
discovery or briefing with respect to those claims, the trial 
court went on to dismiss Petitioners’ taking claims sua 
sponte, finding that those claims accrued on the same date as 
the contract claims. Without any citation to the pleadings, 
the court characterized plaintiffs’ allegations as asserting 
that a taking occurred when the government “passed the 
legislation because the mere passing of the legislation, ipso 
facto, deprives plaintiffs of their vested right to prepay.” 
App. A24. Thus, the court deemed Petitioners’ taking claims 
to allege solely a “facial” taking of their contractual right to 
prepay, which the court determined would have accrued 
upon the passage of the legislation (or, as the court held, 
upon issuance of the implementing regulations).? App. A34. 


Following Franconia, the Court of Federal Claims issued 
a nearly identical decision on the contract claims of the 
Petitioners in Grass Valley Terrace v. United States on April 12, 
2000. The Grass Valley court agreed with the Franconia court 
that the only performance due by the government under the 
prepayment clause was “to keep its promise to allow 
borrowers an unfettered prepayment right.” App. A56; see 


7 Petitioners sought reconsideration of the court’s opinion on a number 
of grounds, including the fact that the court had mistakenly assumed that 
none of the Petitioners had requested to prepay. As an example, 
Petitioners submitted a declaration from Petitioner Phoebe Perri in which 
she detailed her attempts to prepay and the FmHA’s denial of her request. 
App. A157-58. Because there has been no discovery on the subject claims, 
Ms. Perri’s declaration stands as the only evidence in the record on appeal 
concerning Petitioners’ efforts to prepay. The court acknowledged that it 
had misstated the facts, but denied the motion. See 44 Fed. Cl. 315 (1999). 


11 


also id. at 55 (rejecting argument that the contracts obligated 
the government “to accept prepayment”) (emphases added). 
However, unlike the Franconia court, the Grass Valley court 
dismissed only those claims on which the government had 
moved, i.e., Petitioners’ contract claims. 


B. Proceedings Before the Federal Circuit 


Upon appeal from the trial court’s decision in Franconia, 
the Federal Circuit affirmed, holding that ELIHPA 
immediately “eliminated” and “took away” Petitioners’ 
contract rights. App. A10, Al4. The court concluded that 
any breach occurred on the day that the statute was enacted 
(rather than when the regulations were issued, as the trial 
court held). Id. A10. The Federal Circuit also adopted the 
trial court's reasoning that “the note’s prepayment provision 
did not require any performance on the part of the 
government” and that the government thus had no more 
than an intransitive duty “to continue to allow borrowers 
the unfettered right to prepay their loans at any time.” Id. 


The court then disposed of Petitioners’ taking claims, 
seemingly as an after-thought to its holding on the contract 
claims. The court defined Petitioners’ taking claims - 
contrary to their allegations and arguments - as asserting 
solely a taking of their “unfettered right to prepay.” Id. Al4. 
By setting Petitioners’ claims up in this manner, the court 
was driven to the conclusion that, because this right “was 
eliminated by the government with the passage of ELIHPA,” 
Petitioners’ taking claims accrued upon enactment of the 
statute. Id. In so holding, the court made no mention of the 
fact that the statute vests the agency with authority to grant 
prepayment requests in certain circumstances. 

Petitioners sought rehearing in Franconia, based in part 
on the Federal Circuit's prior opinion in Greenbrier v. United 
States, 193 F.3d 1348 (Fed. Cir. 1999). In Greenbrier, the court 


applied the precedent of this Court to hold that the taking 
claims of owners under a materially identical housing 


12 


program accrued, not upon the enactment of ELIHPA, but at 
the later date when the agency rendered a final decision on a 
prepayment request from each owner. The court denied the 
petition for rehearing on June 12, 2001. App. A70. 


Following the issuance of the Federal Circuit's decision 
in Franconia, but before it denied rehearing in that case, the 
court issued a per curium decision in Grass Valley in which it 
affirmed the dismissal of Petitioners’ contract claims on 
statute of limitations grounds. App. A15. Although no 
written opinion accompanied the order, the court's decision 
evidently was based on its holding in Franconia. 


REASONS FOR GRANTING THE PETITION 


This case presents significant legal issues concerning 
when contract and taking claims against the federal 
government accrue for purposes of the statute of limitations 
set forth in the Tucker Act, 28 U.S.C. § 2501. This Court has 
recognized that cases presenting limitations issues are 
important because of the fairness and judicial efficiency 
policies they present. In this case, however, the Federal 
Circuit applied the statute of limitations in a manner that 
raises deeper concerns of fairness and gross judicial 
inefficiency. This case also impacts a large class of regulated 
parties and government contractors. 


. The decision below squarely conflicts with the firmly 
established precedent of this Court in both contract and 
takings cases, thereby upsetting settled expectations and 
creating confusion in the law. This confusion may lead 
other claimants to file suit prematurely to avoid dismissal of 
their claims, imposing an unnecessary burden on the courts. 


* — E.g., Lampf v. Gilbertson, 501 U.S. 350, 357 (1991) (“[L]imitations 
periods in general promote important considerations of fairness.”); Board 
of Regents v. Tomanio, 446 U.S. 478, 487-88 (1980) (statutes of limitations 
“have long been respected as fundamental to a well-ordered judicial 
system”). For similar reasons, the Court has granted certiorari in many 
takings cases involving the ripeness doctrine. See infra at 24-25. 


13 


The decision also exempts the government from standard 
contract principles in a manner that permits claims against 
the government to be dismissed as time-barred, when in any 
other context the claims clearly would be deemed timely. By 
rendering otherwise valid contract rights unenforceable in 
this manner, the decision endangers the government's 
credibility as a reliable business partner. Finally, the 
decision threatens to extinguish the claims of hundreds, 
perhaps thousands, of Section 515 owners across the 
country,? and impacts countless other government 
contractors whose potential claims might be rendered time- 
barred under the Federal Circuit’s unprecedented analysis. 


I. The Federal Circuit Exempted The Government From 
Standard Contract Law Principles By Holding That The 
Legislation Constituted An Immediate Breach Of 
Petitioners’ Prepayment Option. 


“When the United States enters into contract relations, its 
rights and duties therein are governed generally by the law 
applicable to contracts between private individuals.” Mobil 
Oil Exploration & Producing Southeast, Inc. v. United States, 530 
U.S. 604, 607 (2000) (“Mobil”); United States v. Winstar Corp., 
518 U.S. 839, 895 (1996) (“Winstar’).° As this Court held in 


% In addition to Petitioners, there are hundreds of similarly-situated 
owners with claims now pending before the Court of Federal Claims. E.g., 
Adams v. United States, No. 96-699C; Atwood-Leisman v. United States, No. 
98-815C; Allegra Villa v. United States, No. 98-823C; Bellas Domas v. United 
States, No. 98-826C. There are also countless other Section 515 owners in 
the country who have not yet attempted to prepay but may decide to do 
so in the future, who now will be unable to assert any claim against the 
government should their requests to prepay be denied. See Belden & 
Wiener, HOUSING IN RURAL AMERICA 117 (1999) (reporting that by August 
1979, the FmHA had issued 6,874 loans under the Section 515 program). 


10 Accord Clearfield Trust Co. v. United States, 318 U.S. 363, 369 (1943) 
(“The United States does business on business terms.”); Perry v. United 
States, 294 U.S. 330, 352 (1935); Cooke v. United States, 91 U.S. 389, 398 
(1875) (when the United States “comes down from its position of 


14 


Winstar, holding the government accountable to such 
standards is essential to protect “the Government's own 
long-run interest as a reliable contracting partner in the 
myriad workaday transactions of its agencies.” 518 U.S. at 
883. By contrast, a rule “expanding the Government's 
opportunities for contractual abrogation” would have “the 
certain result of undermining the Government's credibility 
at the bargaining table and increasing the cost of its 
engagements.” Id. at 884. The Federal Circuit's decision in 
this case directly creates that very danger, in that it rejects 
contract claims against the government as time-barred when 
indistinguishable claims brought against a private party 
would be deemed timely and heard on their merits.” 


A. The Federal Circuit Improperly Held That The 
Government Can “Eliminate” The Contract Rights 
Of Its Business Partners By Passing Legislation. 


The Federal Circuit held that Petitioners’ contract claims 
accrued upon the enactment of ELIHPA because the statute 
immediately “took away,” “eliminated,” and “conclusively 
abolished” their contract rights. App. Al0, Al4. Thus, the 
court in effect held that the government breached the 
contracts by passing a law. But standard contract law 
principles, recognized by this Court to apply against the 
government, make clear that it was the agency with whom 


sovereignty, and enters the domain of commerce, it submits itself to the 
same laws that govern individuals there”). 


'' Given the unique jurisdiction of the Court of Federal Claims and the 
Federal Circuit, this Court has recognized the need to review cases 
emanating from those courts even in the absence of a split among the 
Circuit Courts. In fact, the Court has in recent years granted certiorari in 
three government contract cases from the Federal Circuit, all involving 
questions of the extent to which the government is bound by standard 
contract law principles. See Mobil, 530 U.S. 604; Winstar, 518 U.S. 839; 
Hercules Inc. v. United States, 514 U.S. 1049 (1995); see also Cardinal Chem. 
Co. v. Morton Int'l, Inc., 508 U.S. 83, 89 (1993) (noting that the rules applied 
by the Federal Circuit are “a matter of special importance to the Nation”). 


15 


Petitioners contracted, and not Congress, that breached the 
contracts. That breach occurred not when the applicable law 
changed, but rather when the FmHA enforced the new 
requirements against Petitioners by failing to accept their 
prepayment requests according to the terms of their 
contracts. 


As the Ninth Circuit recently held, the government was 
not acting in its capacity as the sovereign when it enacted 
ELIHPA. Kimberly Assoc. v. United States, _ F.3d __, 2001 
WL 930454, *4 (9th Cir. Aug. 17, 2001) (publication pending) 
(finding it “unquestionable” that “the government was not 
acting in a ‘public and general’ capacity” when it passed 
ELIHPA, which “constituted a narrow, targeted piece of 
legislation aimed at relieving the government from onerous 
provisions contained in a finite number of specific contracts 
it had already entered”); accord Adams v. United States, 42 
Fed. Cl. 463, 480 (1998) (sovereign acts defense inapplicable 
because ELIHPA “specifically targeted the termination 
options found in FmHA’s contracts”). As a result, the 
government's repudiation of Petitioners’ contracts should 
not be afforded any special status simply because it came in 
the form of legislation.2 The Federal Circuit's contrary 
holding that the legislation eliminated Petitioners’ contract 
rights ipse dixit reflects a significant departure from standard 
contract law principles expressly held by this Court to be 
applicable to the government. 


'2 See Mobil, 530 U.S. at 619-20 (where sovereign acts defense not raised, 
government actions in passing legislation analyzed under standard 
contract principles: “the fact that [the government's) repudiation rested 
upon the enactment of a new statute makes no significant difference”); 
Alaska Pulp Corp. v. United States, 48 Fed. Cl. 655, 659 (2001) (rules 
applicable to a party's repudiation of a contract are “no different where 
the government as a party to the contract commits this same sin through 
subsequent legislation”). Here, as in Mobil, the government cannot rely on 
the sovereign acts doctrine, having failed to raise the argument below. 


16 


B. ELIHPA Constituted An Anticipatory Repudiation 
Of Petitioners’ Prepayment Option, Which Did Not 
Start The Statute Of Limitations. 


One basic principle of contract law is the doctrine of 
anticipatory repudiation, set forth in the Restatement 
(Second) of Contracts § 250. See Mobil, 530 U.S. at 608." The 
Restatement explains that a “repudiation” is a “statement by 
the obligor to the obligee indicating that the obligor will 
commit a breach that would of itself give the obligee a claim 
for damages for total breach.” Id. In response to such a 
statement of prospective nonperformance, the obligee may 
either sue for breach immediately or await the time that the 
obligor’s performance under the contract comes due. 
Williston § 1337; Roehm v. Horst, 178 U.S. 1, 10 (1900) (finding 
it “reasonable to allow an option to the injured party, either 
to sue immediately, or to wait till the time when the act was 
to be done”). If the obligee elects to wait, the statute of 
limitations on a claim for breach does not commence to run 
until the time for performance arrives and the obligor 
actually fails to perform. Kinsey v. United States, 852 F.2d 
556, 558 (Fed. Cir. 1988); 1 Corman, LIMITATION OF ACTIONS 
§ 7.2.1 (1991). 


This Court's decision in Mobil makes clear how the 
doctrine of anticipatory repudiation applies in the context of 


3 Few precepts of contract law are more firmly entrenched than the 
doctrine of anticipatory repudiation, which can be traced back to the 
landmark case of Horchster v. De la Tour, 118 Eng. Rep. 922 (Q.B. 1853). See 
Corbin, CONTRACTS §§ 959, 1053 (1964); Jaeger, WILLISTON ON CONTRACTS 
§§ 824, 1300 et seq., 2027B (3d ed. 1968) (‘Williston’); 17A Am. Jur. 2d, 
Contracts, § 448 et seq. (1990); Rowley, A Brief History Of Anticipatory 
Repudiation In American Contract Law, 69 U. CINN. L. REV. 565 (2001). 


“4 As Professor Corbin explains, this rule serves important policy goals: 
The plaintiff should not be penalized for leaving to the defendant 
an opportunity to retract his wrongful repudiation; and he would 


be so penalized if the statutory period of limitation is held to begin 
to run against him immediately. Corbin, CONTRACTS § 989 (1964). 


17 


legislation passed by Congress. There, the government was 
obligated under contracts with oil companies to approve oil 
exploration plans submitted by the companies, if proper, 
within thirty days after submission. 530 U.S. at 609-10. 
Subsequent legislation, however, placed conditions on that 
approval, making it impossible for the government to 
approve plans submitted by the companies within the 
promised time frame. Id. at 611-12; see also Conoco Inc. v. 
United States, 35 Fed. Cl. 309, 318 (1996). 


The trial court found, based on these facts, that the 
legislation constituted an “anticipatory repudiation” of the 
contracts. Conoco, Inc., 35 Fed. Cl. at 327. This Court agreed, 
holding that “the Government's communication of its intent 
to commit [a] breach amounted to a repudiation of the 
contracts.” Mobil, 530 U.S. at 621, 619 (“[I]f legislation passed 
by Congress and signed by the President is not a ‘statement 
by the obligor,’ it is difficult to imagine what would 
constitute such a statement.”). Thus, the legislation did not 
constitute an immediate failure to perform under the 
contracts, but rather a statement of the government's intent 
to breach the contracts in the future - a classic anticipatory 
repudiation.’ This was true even though the legislation left 
no doubt that the government would not comply with its 
obligations under the contracts. See id. at 611-12, 618. 1 

This Court thus recognized the critical distinction 
between the enactment of legislation by Congress, which 
amounts to only an anticipatory repudiation of affected 


1S The Court's conclusion that the legislation constituted a repudiation 
of the contracts at issue, as opposed to a full breach, was key to its holding 
that the remedy of restitution was available to the contractors. /d. at 608. 
% A “repudiation” is no different from an “anticipatory repudiation.” 
See Restatement (Second) of Contracts § 250 (“repudiation” defined as a 
“statement . . . that the obligor will commit a breach”), quoted in Mobil, 530 
U.S. at 608; Black’s Law Dictionary 1306 (7th ed. 1999) (“repudiation” 
defined as “words or actions that indicate an intention not to perform the 
contract in the future; a threatened breach of contract”). 


18 


contracts, and the later implementation of that legislation by 
the contracting agency, which can result in an actual breach. 
Other courts have reached the same conclusion, both before 
and after Mobil was decided. See Schism v. United States, 239 
F.3d 1280, 1290 (Fed. Cir. 2001); Far West Fed. Bank v. Office of 
Thrift Supervision, 119 F.3d 1358, 1365 (9th Cir. 1997) (quoting 
Restatement § 253); Stone Forrest Indus. v. United States, 973 
F.2d 1548, 1552 (Fed. Cir. 1993); Alaska Pulp Corp., 48 Fed. Cl. 
at 659 (legislation constituted a statement of “prospective 
nonperformance” and implementation by agency resulted in 
an actual breach); Plaintiffs in Winstar-Related Cases, 37 Fed. 
Cl. at 183 (applying this Court's decision in Winstar to hold 
that “FIRREA was essentially a legally binding forecast of a 
future breach” and thus “falls well within the definition of 
an anticipatory repudiation.”). 


The Federal Circuit's holding that ELIHPA constituted 
an immediate breach of Petitioners’ contracts cannot be 
squared with this precedent.!” Just as the contracts in Mobil 
obligated the government to approve proper exploration 
plans submitted by the contractors, the contracts here 
obligated the FmHA to approve prepayment requests 
submitted by the owners. And just as the legislation in 
Mobil announced that the government would not approve 
exploration plans according to the terms of the contracts at 
issue, ELIHPA announced that the government would not 
approve prepayment requests in accordance with 
Petitioners’ contracts. In both cases, the government's 
legislative act of placing extra-contractual conditions on its 
future obligations amounted to a repudiation of the 
contracts, which would ripen into an actual breach only 
when the time for performance by the agency arrives. 


'’ Although Mobil was decided after this case was fully briefed to the 
Federal Circuit in Franconia, Petitioners notified the court of the decision 
pursuant to Fed. R. App. P. 28(j). The Federal Circuit, however, failed to 
discuss or even reference the case in its decision. 


19 


If this were a dispute between private parties, there 
would be no question as to how these principles apply. The 
law is clear that a party’s statement that it will not honor the 
terms of an option contained in a contract constitutes an 
anticipatory repudiation, such that the option-holder need 
not bring suit until after it attempts to exercise the option 
and the repudiating party fails to perform by accepting the 
tender. See Holm v. CalEnergy Co., 229 F.3d 1157, 2000 WL 
975177 at *1-2 (9th Cir. 2000) (table case); Hermanowski v. 
Acton Corp., 580 F. Supp. 140, 143 (E.D.N.Y. 1983), aff'd in 
relevant part, 729 F.2d 921 (2d Cir. 1984); Rossi v. Oristian, 376 
N.Y.S.2d 295, 297 (N.Y. App. Div. 1975) (a claim for breach 
of a stock option contract “which provides that plaintiff 
might exercise the option at any time, presents a situation 
where the statute of limitations does not commence to run 
until the person granted the option demands the stock”). 


This conclusion is mandated by the very nature of a 
contractual option, which vests the option-holder with the 
right to choose at which point during the promised period it 
wishes to exercise the option. A contrary rule allowing the 
limitations period to commence upon the defendant's 
repudiation “would permit the defendant to select the date 
of breach and thus rob the plaintiff of the value of his 
option.” Hermanowski, 580 F. Supp. at 143; see also Fullington 
v. M. Penn Phillips Co., 395 P.2d 124 (Ore. 1964) (the “very 
privilege” for which an option holder bargains is “the 
assurance that he will have the entire option period in which 
to make his decision”). 

The result should be no different here. Petitioners’ 
contracts granted them the option to prepay their mortgages 
at any time that they desired during their fifty-year 
mortgage terms. As a result, the statute of limitations on 
Petitioners’ contract claims did not begin to run until each 
owner attempted to prepay and the government failed to 
perform by accepting each owner's tender of prepayment. 
The Federal Circuit’s contrary holding that every Section 515 


20 


owner affected by ELIHPA was required to file suit within 
six years after enactment of the statute, regardless of 
whether they attempted or even desired to prepay during 
that window of time, improperly permitted the government, _ 
rather than Petitioners, to set the date of breach. In granting 
this power to the government, the court exempted it from 
standard principles of anticipatory repudiation. 


C. The Federal Circuit Created Contract Rules 
Applicable Only To The Government In Holding 
That The FmHA Had No Affirmative Obligation To 
Accept Prepayment Requests Made By Owners. 


The Federal Circuit attempted to avoid the force of this 
precedent by holding that the prepayment option of 
Petitioners’ contracts imposed no performance obligation 
on the government, other than an amorphous duty to 
“continue to allow” owners to prepay. App. Al0. The court 
found that, although Petitioners had the right to prepay their 
mortgages at any time, the government had no reciprocal 
duty to accept such prepayments when tendered by owners. 
Id. (finding that the “prepayment provision did not require 
any performance on the part of the government”). By thus 
relieving the government of any performance under the 
contracts, the court could hold that the statute of limitations 
on Petitioners’ contract claims accrued when the 
government, by passing ELIHPA, ceased keeping its 
promise to “continue to allow” owners to prepay. 


The court's analysis of the parties’ respective obligations 
under the contracts is difficult to understand and harder to 
justify. Indeed, The terms of the contracts, the governing 
statutes and regulations, and the applicable case law all 
make clear what would go without saying if this were a 
dispute between private parties: that the government had 
not only a duty to allow owners to prepay, but also an 
affirmative duty to accept prepayment requests when made 
by owners. 


21 


Petitioners’ contracts state that prepayments “may be 
made at any time” (emphasis added). This clause necessarily 
encompasses both a right on the part of owners to submit 
prepayment requests and a reciprocal obligation on the part 
of the government to accept such requests when made. Any 
other interpretation would render the contracts illusory: 
absent a government obligation to accept prepayment, the 
owners’ contractual right to prepay would be meaningless. 
Mobil, 530 U.S. at 620 (finding government consideration of 
plans submitted by contractors to be a “necessary reciprocal 
obligation” without which the contracts would be rendered 
“illusory”); see also Winstar, 518 U.S. at 921 (Scalia, |» 
concurring) (government contracts should not be read in a 
manner that renders government obligations “illusory”). 


The statutes and regulations governing the Section 515 
program confirm that the government holds an obligation to 
accept prepayment requests made by owners and thereupon 
return the owners’ security (i.e., the property). Both before 
and after ELIHPA was enacted, the government uniformly 
recognized its obligation to “accept” prepayment requests.1 
The regulations in effect when Petitioners entered into their 
contracts also illustrate that the government's duty entails, 
not only an obligation to accept the owner's tender of 
prepayment, but also to complete a litany of tasks necessary 
to process the final payment, release the security, and permit 
the owner to exit the program.! None of these provisions 


18 See Pub. L. No. 96-153, § 503(b), 93 Stat. 1134 (1979), codified at 42 
U.S.C. § 1472(c)(1),(2) (describing when Secretary “may not accept an offer 
to prepay” and when Secretary “shall accept such offer”); 42 USC. 
§ 1472(c)(4)(A),(5)(A) (setting forth agency procedures “before accepting 
any offer to prepay,” when agency “may accept the offer to prepay,” and 
when it “may not accept” any such offer); 7 C.F.R. § 1965.90(b) (1987) 
(outlining when agency “will accept prepayment” and describing agency 
procedures “upon acceptance of the offer to prepay”) (emphases added). 


'° 7CER. § 1965.90(e) (1983) (outlining procedures for final payment 
and release); id. § 1866.2 (1983) (authorizing County Supervisor to execute 
the necessary releases to return the security to the borrower); id. § 1866.3 


22 


would make any sense unless the government actually was 
obligated to “accept” prepayment offers from owners. 2° 


Finally, the court’s holding that the prepayment clause 
“did not require any performance on the part of the 
government” yet again defies ordinary contract principles. 
Cases involving contractual option provisions consistently 
hold that once an option-holder exercises its option, the 
other party has a duty to perform by accepting the tender. 
Holm, 2000 WL 975177 at *1 (“obligation” to sell stock arose 
when option-holder “tendered the option price”); Saewitz v. 
Epstein, 6 F.Supp.2d 151, 157 (N.D.N.Y. 1998) (“the actual 
exercise of an option contract by the optionee is the 
performance of a condition precedent to the optionor’s duty 
to perform”) (citing 4 Corbin, CONTRACTS § 264 (1951)); see 
also Lucente v. IBM Corp., 146 F.Supp.2d 298, 309 (S.D.N.Y. 
2001) (option-holder “has sole power to decide when (or if) 
the optionor must perform”). Here, too, the government 
was obligated to perform by accepting prepayment if and 
when each owner tendered a prepayment request, and then 
releasing the security held by the government. 


By finding that the contracts obligated the government to 
do no more than simply “continue to allow” owners to 
prepay, the court was able to hold that the only promise the 
government made under the contracts was to keep its 
promise. See App. A10; see also id. at A31. This reasoning, if 
generally applied, would write the doctrine of anticipatory . 
repudiation out of the law, in that any statement by a party 


(1983) (detailing procedures for the county office to follow in handling 
final payment and delivering of documents to the borrower). - 


20 In fact, the Federal Circuit held in Greenbrier, with respect to the 
sections of ELIHPA applicable to HUD, that the statute does not disallow 
owners from prepaying, but rather imposes conditions on the agency's 
ability to accept requests to prepay. 193 F.3d at 1357 (holding that “neither 
statute denies an owner the ability to prepay its mortgage loan; rather, 
both statutes provide that an owner may prepay, albeit in accordance with 
a plan of action approved by the Secretary”). 


23 


of its intention not to perform under a contract could be 
converted into a breach of the duty to “keep the promise,”?! 
so that the statute of limitations would begin to run 
immediately when the statement is made. Fortunately, the 
law of anticipatory repudiation avoids such absurd results 
by providing that the limitations period comunences only 
when the repudiating party actually fails to perform. See 
supra at 17, 20. 


II. The Federal Circuit Defied This Court’s Precedent By 
Holding That Petitioners’ Taking Claims Accrued 
Upon The Enactment Of Legislation That Expressly 
Left Open The Possibility That Petitioners Would Be 
Permitted To Use Their Properties As They Desired. 


A. The Legislation At Issue Demands An “As 
Applied” Takings Analysis. 


This Court has long held that a taking claim based on a 
statute or regulation that allows for a discretionary agency 
decision on the scope of permissible use of the property at 
issue is not ripe for review until the agency renders such a 
decision. Palazzolo v. Rhode Island, 121 S. Ct. 2448, 2458 
(2001); Suitum v. Tahoe Reg’l Planning Agency, 520 U.S. 725, 
735-39 (1997); MacDonald, Sommer & Frates v. Yolo County, 
477 US. 340, 348 (1986); Williamson County Reg’l Planning 
Comm'n v. Hamilton Bank, 473 U.S. 172, 193 (1985). Thus, 
where federal legislation regulates the use of property but 
provides an escape hatch permitting the agency to exempt 
certain property from its requirements, a takings claim is not 
ripe until the agency applies the legislation to the property 
in question. Otherwise, the agency’s exercise of the 
discretion afforded to it under the statute to determine 


21 Indeed, the notion of a promise not to repudiate is merely a legal 
fiction, since no such promise is ever expressly made. See 5 Corbin, 
CONTRACTS § 1053 (1964) (“A forbearance to repudiate is never specifically 
promised.”). Remarkably, the Federal Circuit converted this legal fiction 
into the government's only duty under the contracts. 


24 


whether the owner will be permitted to use the property as 
desired would be undermined. 


In Hodel v. Virginia Surface Mining and Reclamation Ass’n, 
Inc., 452 U.S. 264, 295 (1981), for example, owners of coal 
mining lands alleged that the “mere enactment” of the 
Surface Mining Control and Reclamation Act effected a 
taking of their properties. Noting that the statute did not 
“categorically prohibit” the owner’s intended use of the 
property, the Court held that the owners’ taking claim was 
unripe. Id. at 297. The Court added that its holding did not 
preclude the owners from arguing that the statute “as 
applied” to their property resulted in a taking. Id. at 297 
n.40; see also United States v. Riverside Bayview Homes, Inc., 474 
U.S. 121, 126-27 (1985) (holding that the government's 
assertion of jurisdiction over wetlands did not in and of 
itself amount to a taking because under the permitting 
scheme created, “permission may be granted, leaving the 
landowner free to use the property as desired”).2 


The application of this precedent to the statute at issue is 
clear-cut. ELIHPA provides that prepayment requests may 
be accepted without any restrictions when the Secretary of 
Agriculture “determines that housing opportunities of 
minorities will not be materially affected as a result of the 
prepayment” and that “there is an adequate supply of safe, 
decent, and affordable rental housing within the market area 
of the housing and related facilities and sufficient actions 
have been taken to ensure that the rental housing will be 
made available to each tenant upon displacement.” 42 


2 The Courts of Appeal uniformly have recognized these principles and 
applied them in a variety of contexts. See, e.g., Gulf Power Co. v. FCC, 208 
F.3d 1263, 1271-72 (11th Cir. 2000); Gilbert v. Cambridge, 932 F.2d 51, 55-57 
(1st Cir. 1991) (claim unripe where scheme created by ordinance left open 
possibility “that landlords may be granted leave to use their property as 
they wish”); cf. National Adver. Co. v. Raleigh, “47 F.2d 1158, 1166 (4th Cir. 
1991) (taking claim accrued upon enactment of ordnance that demanded 
“immediate compliance without exception”). 


25 


U.S.C. § 1472(c)(5)(G)(ii) (App. A89). If the Secretary makes 
these determinations in his or her discretion, the restrictions 
imposed by ELIHPA do not apply and the owner will be 
permitted to terminate its participation in the program.” 


Thus, there can be no doubt, under the clear precedent of 
this Court, that Petitioners’ taking claims did not accrue 
upon the “mere enactment” of ELIHPA. Because the 
legislation grants FmHA the discretion to grant prepayment 
requests without restrictions and thereby permit owners to 
use their properties as they desire in certain circumstances, 
Petitioners’ taking claims present “as-applied” takings that 
could become ripe for review only upon implementation of 
the new requirements by the agency. As such, Petitioners’ 
taking claims did not accrue until that time. 


Remarkably, the Federal Circuit recognized as much in a 
recent case involving a similar housing program that was 
impacted by a different section of ELIHPA. In Greenbrier v. 
United States, 193 F.3d 1348 (Fed. Cir. 1999), owners of low- 
income housing regulated by the Department of Housing 


2% Regulations issued under the statute similarly provide that “[i]n 
certain instances, prepayment may be approved” without any conditions 
imposed on the owner. 7 C.F.R. § 1965.215(c). In particular, “prepayment 
may be accepted without restrictions” where, among other things, “there 
is no longer a need for the housing.” Id. § 1965.215(c)(3),(iii). App. A92-94. 


24 «It nearly goes without saying that Petitioners’ taking claims could not 
have accrued until they became ripe for review. Levald, Inc. v. Palm Dessert, 
998 F.2d 680, 687 (9th Cir. 1993) (“Determining when the cause of action 
accrued is merely the corollary to the ripeness inquiry.”); Biddison v. 
Chicago, 921 F.2d 724, 728-29 (7th Cir. 1991) (holding that “a taking claim 
accrues at the same time that it ripens”); Norco Construction v. King County, 
801 F.2d 1143, 1146 (9th Cir. 1986) (holding that “the same considerations 
that render a claim premature prevent accrual of a claim for limitations 
purposes”); Plaintiffs in Winstar-Related Cases v. United States, 37 Fed. Cl. 
174, 182 (1997) (“For a cause of action to accrue and the statute of 
limitations to run, the claim must be ripe. Common sense, as much as 
case law, dictates this maxim, for the alternative would lead plaintiffs into 
a conundrum in which their claims could be time-barred before even 
becoming amenable to judicial resolution.”). 


26 


and Urban Development (“HUD”) challenged provisions of 
ELIHPA and a 1990 statute that restricted their right to 
prepay their mortgages. Observing that the legislation 
“expressly granted [HUD] authority to approve prepayment 
proposals” in some instances, the Federal Circuit held that 
the taking claims before it were unripe because none of the 
owners had alleged that they sought HUD’s approval. Id. at 
1358-59. Thus, while the Federal Circuit held that the taking 
claims of the HUD owners in Greenbrier were filed too early, 
it held that the materially indistinguishable taking claims of 
the FmHA owners in Franconia were filed too late. There is 
no basis for the Federal Circuit's decision to follow this 
Court’s precedent in Greenbrier and then ignore it in 
Franconia. 


B. The Decision Below Sidesteps This Court's 
Precedent By Ignoring The Language Of The 
Statute And Misstating Petitioners’ Claims. 


The Federal Circuit was able to ignore these firmly 
entrenched principles of takings law only by turning a blind 
eye to the terms of the statute. In particular, the court 
disregarded the provisions of the statute that permit FmHA 
to accept prepayment requests without restriction where it 
determines, among other things, that there is no longer a 
need for the housing. By reading this exception out of the 
terms of the statute, the court was able to treat Petitioners’ 
claims as asserting only a facial taking. Had the court 
heeded the fact that the statute permits prepayment without 
restriction in some cases, it would have had no choice but to 
analyze Petitioners’ taking claims on an as-applied basis.” 


% The court did not discuss the Greenbrier decision or explain why its 
holding was not controlling in this case. The only similarity between the 
two cases is that the plaintiffs’ claims were dismissed in both. 

% While the court did not use the phrase “facial taking” per se, it held 
that the statute “took away and conclusively abolished” Petitioners’ 
property interests upon its enactment. App. A14. 


27 


The Federal Circuit overlooked not only the terms of the 
statute and its own precedent, but also the substance of 
Petitioners’ allegations. Petitioners alleged that the 
government's conduct effected a taking of their properties as 
of the date that Petitioners actually would have terminated 
their participation in the program but for the restrictions 
imposed by the legislation.” Thus, Petitioners alleged that 
the agency’s application of the legislation, and not its mere 
enactment, effected a taking. 


Moreover, even if Petitioners had not clearly alleged an 
as-applied taking of their properties, the Federal Circuit still 
would have been bound by the nature of the statute to 
employ an as-applied analysis. In cases where a facial 
taking claim is precluded by the fact that the statute does not 
categorically prohibit the owner's desired use of the 
property, the proper inquiry is whether the claim is ripe 
under an as-applied analysis. See, e.g., Hodel, 452 U.S. at 295- 
97 & n40; Pennell v. San Jose, 485 U.S. 1, 9-10 (1988); 
Greenbrier, 193 F.3d at 1358-59 (rejecting facial taking claim 
and then proceeding to as-applied taking analysis); see also 
Riverside Bayview Homes, Inc., 474 U.S. at 126-27 (1985). In 
this case, however, the Federal Circuit ignored the language 
of the statute, applied only a facial taking analysis, and 
concluded that the claims are time-barred. 


The court’s analysis is not saved by its characterization 
of Petitioners’ claims as alleging a taking of their contractual 
prepayment right. App. Al14. First, the notion that the 
property interest upon which Petitioners base their taking 
claim is an “unfettered right to prepay” finds no source in 


27 See App. A132, A155 (“As of the date of Government performance 
required by each contract entered into between defendant and the 
plaintiffs, defendant's conduct constitutes a taking of plaintiffs’ i 
for public use . . .”); App. A132, A154 (identifying the “date of 
Government performance” as “the date that each plaintiff would 
terminate its contract but for the Government's repudiation”); App. A157- 
58 (statement of Petitioner that she did not suffer any damages until the 
agency denied her request to prepay her mortgag, 


28 


Petitioners’ pleadings. Rather, the record makes clear that 
Petitioners, as title owners of the land and buildings at issue, 
assert a taking of their state-created real estate interests.” 
Second, as the Federal Circuit recognized in Greenbrier, 193 
F.3d at 1356-59, even if Petitioners alleged solely a taking of 
their contract right to prepay, the possibility that 
prepayment requests may be accepted without restrictions 
in some cases precluded the court from limiting its inquiry 
to whether the enactment of the statute constituted a taking. 


The Federal Circuit in effect held that Petitioners - and, 
by extension, every owner in the Section 515 program - 
should have filed their claims by February 1994 (six years 
after passage of ELIHPA), regardless of whether they had 
any intention of terminating their participation in the 
program by that time. The holding thus creates a new 
acerual standard for taking claims, providing that a statute 
can effect a taking before it has any impact on the use of the 
property at issue. As a result, the decision creates a 
conundrum for other property owners. Previously, owners 
like Petitioners would have been compelled by the 
precedent of this Court to refrain from filing suit until (1) 
they decided to terminate their participation in the program, 
and (2) the agency determined that the owner could not, at 
that time, meet the statutory requirements for doing so. 
Under the holding in Franconia, however, owners facing 
similar circumstances now will be forced to file suit 
prematurely in order to avoid dismissal of their claims for 
filing too late. 29 . 


% See App. A129, 151 (alleging that the government effected “a taking of 
plaintiffs’ properties for public use” by, among other things, “requiring 
plaintiffs to house low-income tenants”) (emphasis added). Petitioners’ 
interests in their properties include the right to economically productive 
use and enjoyment, the right to exclusive possession, and the rights to 
transfer, devise, and dispose of their properties. 


% Yet if they do so, they may face dismissal of their claims for filing too 
early, under the ripeness doctrine, based on Greenbrier and this Court's 
precedent - a veritable Catch-22. See Bayou des Familles Development Corp. 


29 


III. The Decision Below Creates Unworkable And Illogical 
Rules Governing The Accrual Of Claims Against The 
Government. 


If permitted to stand, the Federal Circuit's holding will 
create unjust and unmanageable results for parties and the 
courts. Take for example a typical owner who entered into 
the program in 1979 and who, after twenty years, was 
financially able to prepay its mortgage in 1999. Assuming 
that the conditions for prepaying without restrictions could 
not be met at that time, the agency would deny the owner's 
prepayment request. If the owner then desired to file suit 
against the government for its failure to honor the terms of 
the contract, or for an improper taking, the owner would 
find that its claims had accrued eleven years earlier in 1988 
and became time-barred five years earlier in 1994. The 
decision below thus would compel this owner to file suit by 
1994, long before its taking claim became ripe, indeed, well 
before it even had any desire to exercise its prepayment 
option, and, consequently, before it suffered any actual 
damages. The law should not require such bizarre results. 


CONCLUSION 


This Court should grant certiorari in this case to 
determine, where the government attempts to modify its 
contracts through legislation, when the statute of limitations 
under the Tucker Act begins to run. 


v. United States, 130 F.3d 1034, 1037-38 (Fed. Cir. 1997) (observing that the 
government “uses the ripeness doctrine as both a sword and a shield”). 
Indeed, if Petitioners had brought their taking claims immediately upon 
enactment of ELIHPA, the government undoubtedly would have 
challenged those claims as unripe, given the possibility of unrestricted 
prepayment under the statute. Surely, the Takings Clause should not be 
the subject of such gamesmanship. As this Court has stated, “[w]e see no 
reason why the Takings Clause of the Fifth Amendment, as much a part of 
the Bill of Rights as the First Amendment or Fourth Amendment, should 
be relegated to the status of a poor relation ....” Dolan v. City of Tigard, 
512 U.S. 374, 392 (1994).

FRANCONIA ASSOCIATES, DAVID HODGES, SR. C/O

SCENIC VALLEY ASSOCIATES, VALLEY ASSOCIATES, 

DUBLIN PLAZA, INC., EVERGREEN MANOR 
ASSOCIATES, FOX RIDGE PHASE II, MARILYN GRAHAM,
